========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is that I’ve been having a really hard time with work that takes sustained brain effort — like sitting down and really thinking through monthly reports, or planning something more involved like a multi-site health event. It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.

But when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. I’ll put it off, switch to something easier, tell myself I’ll come back to it when I have a clearer stretch of time, and then suddenly it’s later and I still haven’t done the main thing. It’s been frustrating, honestly, because these aren’t optional tasks in my job, and I’m starting to feel like I need help figuring out why I keep avoiding them even when I know better.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.

Like, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.

And when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. But apparently I’m sort of skimming without realizing I’m skimming, and then the errors show up once it’s already in someone else’s inbox. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.

I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.

And it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is that I’ve been having a really hard time with work that takes sustained brain effort — like sitting down and really thinking through monthly reports, or planning something more involved like a multi-site health event. It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.\n\nBut when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. I’ll put it off, switch to something easier, tell myself I’ll come back to it when I have a clearer stretch of time, and then suddenly it’s later and I still haven’t done the main thing. It’s been frustrating, honestly, because these aren’t optional tasks in my job, and I’m starting to feel like I need help figuring out why I keep avoiding them even when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty paying attention to details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. But apparently I’m sort of skimming without realizing I’m skimming, and then the errors show up once it’s already in someone else’s inbox. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.

Monthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.

It’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I start talking too much when I’m nervous about having dropped the ball, like I’ll over-explain a scheduling mistake and keep going even after the other person clearly got the point.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers told my parents I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework unless my mother checked my backpack every night.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting by at work and in the community, but people have had to cover gaps for me, deadlines have slipped, and I’ve embarrassed myself with last-minute apologies to coworkers and friends.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 11 months, since my friend died and I took on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S006\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty paying attention to details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. But apparently I’m sort of skimming without realizing I’m skimming, and then the errors show up once it’s already in someone else’s inbox. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I start talking too much when I’m nervous about having dropped the ball, like I’ll over-explain a scheduling mistake and keep going even after the other person clearly got the point.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers told my parents I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework unless my mother checked my backpack every night.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting by at work and in the community, but people have had to cover gaps for me, deadlines have slipped, and I’ve embarrassed myself with last-minute apologies to coworkers and friends.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 11 months, since my friend died and I took on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S006\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as a particularly restless person in the way I imagine that — like, I’m not pacing around all the time or feeling like I have to be moving constantly.\n\nI mean, I probably shift around or tap a pen sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed it as a major thing. If I’m sitting in a meeting or at home, I can sit there physically. The bigger issue for me has been more what’s going on in my head than feeling like I need to get up and move. But if you mean little things like adjusting in my chair or messing with something in my hands, maybe? I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”

I remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.

So yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is that I’ve been having a really hard time with work that takes sustained brain effort — like sitting down and really thinking through monthly reports, or planning something more involved like a multi-site health event. It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.\n\nBut when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. I’ll put it off, switch to something easier, tell myself I’ll come back to it when I have a clearer stretch of time, and then suddenly it’s later and I still haven’t done the main thing. It’s been frustrating, honestly, because these aren’t optional tasks in my job, and I’m starting to feel like I need help figuring out why I keep avoiding them even when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty paying attention to details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.

And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.

But it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.

Outside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.

And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as a particularly restless person in the way I imagine that — like, I’m not pacing around all the time or feeling like I have to be moving constantly.\n\nI mean, I probably shift around or tap a pen sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed it as a major thing. If I’m sitting in a meeting or at home, I can sit there physically. The bigger issue for me has been more what’s going on in my head than feeling like I need to get up and move. But if you mean little things like adjusting in my chair or messing with something in my hands, maybe? I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.\n\nSo yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.

I don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.

After he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.

It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. The place I really get stuck is when something requires real sit-down, sustained brain work. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.

That’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. Then I’ll put it off — sometimes by doing smaller things that feel productive, sometimes by telling myself I’ll be in a better headspace later — and then later keeps getting pushed.

So yes, I do bounce around some, but the bigger issue is that tasks that take mental effort feel almost… blocked. Not because I don’t understand them, and not because I think they’re unimportant. It’s more like I can feel the amount of focus it’s going to take, and my brain just wants to avoid it. Then it becomes this whole cycle of delay and pressure and guilt.
=====================================

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is that I’ve been having a really hard time with work that takes sustained brain effort — like sitting down and really thinking through monthly reports, or planning something more involved like a multi-site health event. It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.\n\nBut when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. I’ll put it off, switch to something easier, tell myself I’ll come back to it when I have a clearer stretch of time, and then suddenly it’s later and I still haven’t done the main thing. It’s been frustrating, honestly, because these aren’t optional tasks in my job, and I’m starting to feel like I need help figuring out why I keep avoiding them even when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty paying attention to details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. But apparently I’m sort of skimming without realizing I’m skimming, and then the errors show up once it’s already in someone else’s inbox. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as a particularly restless person in the way I imagine that — like, I’m not pacing around all the time or feeling like I have to be moving constantly.\n\nI mean, I probably shift around or tap a pen sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed it as a major thing. If I’m sitting in a meeting or at home, I can sit there physically. The bigger issue for me has been more what’s going on in my head than feeling like I need to get up and move. But if you mean little things like adjusting in my chair or messing with something in my hands, maybe? I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.\n\nSo yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving from one thing to another without really finishing anything, or is it more that you just have trouble starting tasks in the first place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.\n\nThat’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving from one thing to another without really finishing anything, or is it more that you just have trouble starting tasks in the first place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. The place I really get stuck is when something requires real sit-down, sustained brain work. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.\n\nThat’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. Then I’ll put it off — sometimes by doing smaller things that feel productive, sometimes by telling myself I’ll be in a better headspace later — and then later keeps getting pushed.\n\nSo yes, I do bounce around some, but the bigger issue is that tasks that take mental effort feel almost… blocked. Not because I don’t understand them, and not because I think they’re unimportant. It’s more like I can feel the amount of focus it’s going to take, and my brain just wants to avoid it. Then it becomes this whole cycle of delay and pressure and guilt.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your personal relationships due to your difficulties with attention and task completion?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I have. I mean, not in the sense that my relationships have completely fallen apart or anything dramatic like that. I’m still involved with people, I still show up, I’m still part of things in the community. But it does affect the way people experience me, I think.

There have been plenty of times where someone is waiting on me to do something or respond to something, and I either forget, or I think I’ll get to it later, and then suddenly it’s the last minute. Then I’m sending these apologetic messages like, “I’m sorry, I meant to follow up,” or “I know I said I’d handle that,” and it’s embarrassing. It makes me feel like I’m putting other people in the position of having to remind me or cover for me.

At work, coworkers have definitely had to fill in gaps at times when deadlines slipped or I didn’t finish something when I said I would. And with friends, it’s more like… I worry they see me as unreliable, even if they don’t say it that directly. I don’t think people hate me for it, but I can tell it creates frustration, and I feel bad about that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is that I’ve been having a really hard time with work that takes sustained brain effort — like sitting down and really thinking through monthly reports, or planning something more involved like a multi-site health event. It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.\n\nBut when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. I’ll put it off, switch to something easier, tell myself I’ll come back to it when I have a clearer stretch of time, and then suddenly it’s later and I still haven’t done the main thing. It’s been frustrating, honestly, because these aren’t optional tasks in my job, and I’m starting to feel like I need help figuring out why I keep avoiding them even when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty paying attention to details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that? Because it’s right there. It’s not that I didn’t care or I was trying to rush through it on purpose. I genuinely thought I had reviewed it. But apparently I’m sort of skimming without realizing I’m skimming, and then the errors show up once it’s already in someone else’s inbox. It’s embarrassing, honestly, especially at work because I’m supposed to be the person coordinating those details.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these difficulties with attention and task completion only at work, or are you also having similar problems in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not only at work. Work is where it’s most visible, because there are deadlines and other people are depending on me, so the consequences show up faster. But it spills into other areas too.\n\nI mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart — but I’m getting by with a lot more scrambling than people probably realize. At work, coworkers have had to cover gaps for me when I didn’t finish something when I said I would, or when I left something too late and then needed someone else to jump in. Deadlines have slipped, and then I’m sending those awful last-minute apology emails like, “Sorry, I know I said I’d have this to you yesterday,” and I hate that.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too, just in a more personal way. With community stuff or friends, I’ll mean to follow through on something, and then I’m late responding or I miss some piece of it, and I end up apologizing at the last minute. It’s embarrassing because I feel like people see me as unreliable, even though in my head I’m trying really hard to keep up. So, no, it’s not contained to work. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks that require sustained brain effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, overwhelmed is a good word for it. It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. There are all these moving parts, and instead of just starting, I’ll sort of freeze and start thinking, okay, I need a clearer block of time for this, or I’ll do it after I handle these smaller things first. And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as a particularly restless person in the way I imagine that — like, I’m not pacing around all the time or feeling like I have to be moving constantly.\n\nI mean, I probably shift around or tap a pen sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed it as a major thing. If I’m sitting in a meeting or at home, I can sit there physically. The bigger issue for me has been more what’s going on in my head than feeling like I need to get up and move. But if you mean little things like adjusting in my chair or messing with something in my hands, maybe? I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.\n\nSo yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.\n\nSo yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving from one thing to another without really finishing anything, or is it more that you just have trouble starting tasks in the first place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. The place I really get stuck is when something requires real sit-down, sustained brain work. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.\n\nThat’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. Then I’ll put it off — sometimes by doing smaller things that feel productive, sometimes by telling myself I’ll be in a better headspace later — and then later keeps getting pushed.\n\nSo yes, I do bounce around some, but the bigger issue is that tasks that take mental effort feel almost… blocked. Not because I don’t understand them, and not because I think they’re unimportant. It’s more like I can feel the amount of focus it’s going to take, and my brain just wants to avoid it. Then it becomes this whole cycle of delay and pressure and guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships due to your difficulties with attention and task completion?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, not in the sense that my relationships have completely fallen apart or anything dramatic like that. I’m still involved with people, I still show up, I’m still part of things in the community. But it does affect the way people experience me, I think.\n\nThere have been plenty of times where someone is waiting on me to do something or respond to something, and I either forget, or I think I’ll get to it later, and then suddenly it’s the last minute. Then I’m sending these apologetic messages like, “I’m sorry, I meant to follow up,” or “I know I said I’d handle that,” and it’s embarrassing. And with friends, it’s more like… I worry they see me as unreliable, even if they don’t say it that directly. Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve noticed as a pattern.

It’s not that I don’t know it matters. I do. Sometimes it matters a lot, and I’m responsible for it.\n\nBut when I get to those tasks, it’s like I hit a wall. I’ll open the document or look at the planning notes, and then I just… stall. And it’s not usually huge, dramatic mistakes, which almost makes it more frustrating because they’re the kinds of things I feel like I should be catching.\n\nLike, I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule thinking I looked it over, and then someone will email me back and say, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Thursday?” or “This person is listed in two places.” Or I’ll send something out for my son’s soccer league — an email to parents or a schedule — and I’ll feel like I checked it, but then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left off some detail that everyone needs.\n\nAnd when it happens, I usually think, how did I miss that?
