And then I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the part that bothers me. I do care, and I know people are depending on me. But those tasks feel so mentally heavy that I avoid them even when I know avoiding them is going to make everything worse later.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, even when you're not actively trying to accomplish a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as a particularly restless person in the way I imagine that — like, I’m not pacing around all the time or feeling like I have to be moving constantly.\n\nI mean, I probably shift around or tap a pen sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed it as a major thing. If I’m sitting in a meeting or at home, I can sit there physically. The bigger issue for me has been more what’s going on in my head than feeling like I need to get up and move. But if you mean little things like adjusting in my chair or messing with something in my hands, maybe? I just haven’t paid as much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with attention and task completion at any point in your life before your current job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early, honestly. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. She basically had to go through it with me and find the permission slips or the homework sheets or whatever I was supposed to be keeping track of.\n\nSo yes, I’d say this isn’t totally new with my current job. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I’m usually more the opposite, where I overthink or get stuck before doing something.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I have moments where I answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really thought through whether I have time for it, but I don’t know that I’d call myself impulsive overall. It’s not something I’ve noticed as a pattern the way I notice the attention and follow-through issues.\n\nWhen you say “act on impulse,” do you mean things like interrupting people, spending money, making sudden decisions, or more just day-to-day stuff? Because if it’s the bigger things, I don’t think that really fits me very well.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and task completion caused significant problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving from one thing to another without really finishing anything, or is it more that you just have trouble starting tasks in the first place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. The place I really get stuck is when something requires real sit-down, sustained brain work. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.\n\nThat’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. Then I’ll put it off — sometimes by doing smaller things that feel productive, sometimes by telling myself I’ll be in a better headspace later — and then later keeps getting pushed.\n\nSo yes, I do bounce around some, but the bigger issue is that tasks that take mental effort feel almost… blocked. Not because I don’t understand them, and not because I think they’re unimportant. It’s more like I can feel the amount of focus it’s going to take, and my brain just wants to avoid it. Then it becomes this whole cycle of delay and pressure and guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships due to your difficulties with attention and task completion?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, not in the sense that my relationships have completely fallen apart or anything dramatic like that. I’m still involved with people, I still show up, I’m still part of things in the community. But it does affect the way people experience me, I think.\n\nThere have been plenty of times where someone is waiting on me to do something or respond to something, and I either forget, or I think I’ll get to it later, and then suddenly it’s the last minute. Then I’m sending these apologetic messages like, “I’m sorry, I meant to follow up,” or “I know I said I’d handle that,” and it’s embarrassing. It makes me feel like I’m putting other people in the position of having to remind me or cover for me.\n\nAt work, coworkers have definitely had to fill in gaps at times when deadlines slipped or I didn’t finish something when I said I would. And with friends, it’s more like… I worry they see me as unreliable, even if they don’t say it that directly. I don’t think people hate me for it, but I can tell it creates frustration, and I feel bad about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve noticed as a pattern.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if a conversation is moving fast or I’m worried I’ll lose my thought, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who constantly talks over them. I’m usually more likely to hold back or wait, especially in work settings, because I’m aware of not wanting to come across as rude.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t ever been formally diagnosed with it before. This is really the first time I’ve sat down with someone specifically to look at it in that way.\n\nAs far as family goes… I’m not really sure. Nobody in my family has talked about having that diagnosis, at least not that I know of. But my family also isn’t the kind of family where people would necessarily go get evaluated for something like that or use those terms, especially older relatives. So I can’t say there’s a known family history. It’s more that I don’t know either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying seated for a long time?\n\nPatient: Not really in a major way, no. I mean, I can sit through meetings or appointments without feeling like I physically have to get up and leave. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly pacing or bouncing out of my chair.\n\nThat said, if something goes on for a long time, I might shift around, change positions, maybe tap my foot a little or look for an excuse to stretch, but I always thought of that as pretty normal. It’s not usually the physical restlessness that stands out to me. I’m more likely to look calm on the outside, I think. So if you mean like feeling driven to move or unable to stay seated, I wouldn’t say that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty engaging in activities quietly, such as reading or doing homework?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being noisy or disruptive while doing things.\n\nIf I’m reading or doing something on my own, I’m usually pretty quiet outwardly. I’m not someone who hums loudly or talks through everything or makes a lot of noise while I’m working. I might shift around or sigh or get up to get a drink, but I don’t think people around me would say, “He can’t do things quietly.”\n\nWith reading, if anything, the issue is more internal than external — but in terms of the question you’re asking, like whether I have trouble engaging in quiet activities quietly, I’d say no, not really. I can sit quietly. I may not always be as productive as I want, but I’m not loud or physically disruptive about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with attention, impulsivity, or hyperactivity as a child, aside from what your teachers mentioned about daydreaming and losing worksheets?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, aside from the daydreaming and losing worksheets, the thing that really stands out from when I was little is that whole pattern around schoolwork and homework. Teachers would tell my parents that I was bright, like I understood the material and could do well when I actually did the work, but I was always sort of mentally somewhere else.\n\nAnd the homework thing was a big deal. It wasn’t always that I didn’t do it — sometimes I did it and then just didn’t turn it in, or I’d forget it was in my folder, or it would be crumpled somewhere in my backpack. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure I had the worksheets, make sure I knew what was due, and make sure it actually got put back where I could find it the next day.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say there were attention-related problems going back to elementary school. I don’t remember being especially hyper or getting in trouble for running around or anything like that, but the forgetfulness, the daydreaming, and needing my mom to stay on top of basic school stuff were definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are fidgeting or restless, even when you are sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I do move around a bit when I’m sitting — like adjusting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, maybe tapping a pen or my fingers if I’m holding something — but I don’t usually experience it as this strong inner restlessness where I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin.\n\nIf I’m in a long meeting or sitting through something that drags on, I might notice myself shifting more, or I’ll want to stand up and stretch, but I’ve always thought of that as kind of normal after sitting too long. It’s not like I’m constantly aware of needing to move.\n\nSo… maybe mild fidgeting sometimes, but not often in a way that feels like a major problem. I think if you asked people who know me, they’d probably say I seem pretty still most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m especially impulsive in that way.\n\nI might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone is going, especially if it’s a casual conversation and I’m trying to be helpful, but I don’t think I’m someone who constantly interrupts people or blurts things out before I can stop myself. In meetings at work, for example, I’m usually pretty careful about waiting my turn, partly because I don’t want to come across as rude or like I’m not listening.\n\nWith decisions in general, I tend to think things through more than act suddenly. Sometimes probably too much, honestly. So if the question is whether impulsiveness is a frequent daily problem for me, I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know that I’d say “frequently,” no. I’m trying to think of what that feels like for me, because when I’m not doing something, I can get sort of… mentally uncomfortable, maybe bored or like I should be doing something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as internal restlessness.

It’s like the moment I know something is going to take real sustained thinking — not just answering a quick email or checking off some obvious task, but actually sitting down and using my brain for a while — I can feel myself almost bracing against it.\n\nMonthly reports are a big example. I’ll know they matter, and I’ll know I need to get them done, but when I open the file it’s like I hit a wall. Same with planning something more involved, like a multi-site health event. I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. The job has made it harder to compensate for, but when I think about school, there were definitely signs of the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. At least not in the obvious way, like making reckless decisions or blurting out major things without thinking. I mean, I’m still functioning — I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart or I can’t manage basic life. I go to work, I take care of things, I show up in the community and with people. From the outside, I probably look like I’m mostly getting by.\n\nBut it has caused problems, yes. People end up kind of filling in the gaps for me more than I’d like to admit. At work, coworkers have reminded me about things or stepped in when I was late getting something done, and I’ve had deadlines slip because I misjudged how long something would take or I just didn’t get to it in the organized way I meant to. And then I’m doing that awful last-minute apology thing — like, “I’m sorry, I know this was due,” or “I’m sorry, I thought I had already sent that,” and it’s embarrassing because it makes me feel unreliable, even though I’m not trying to be.\n\nOutside of work it’s similar, just maybe less formal. Friends will be waiting on me to respond to something, or I’ll say I’m going to take care of something and then I realize too late that I didn’t. I’ve had to apologize at the last minute more times than I’m comfortable with. It’s not that people have cut me off or anything, but I can tell it wears on them, and honestly it wears on me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and task completion consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s been most noticeable for about… just under two years, I guess. Around a year and eleven months. It really picked up after my friend died and I ended up taking on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\nI don’t want to say nothing existed before that, because I’ve already mentioned there were things in school and earlier in life, but in terms of this level of difficulty — where keeping up with tasks and finishing things started feeling like a real ongoing problem — that’s when it got worse. Before that, I think I had more structure or fewer moving pieces, so I could kind of cover for it better.\n\nAfter he passed, there was just more on my plate, and some of it was emotionally heavy too, so suddenly I was responsible for more coordination, more follow-through, more people depending on me. That’s when I really started noticing, “Okay, this isn’t just me having an off week.” It’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving from one thing to another without really finishing anything, or is it more that you just have trouble starting tasks in the first place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the starting part, I think — or maybe not even starting exactly, because I can start little things. The place I really get stuck is when something requires real sit-down, sustained brain work. Like monthly reports, or planning a multi-site health event where I have to think through all the moving parts, coordinate details, make a plan, and then keep coming back to it.\n\nThat’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here. I’ll know it matters, I’ll know people are waiting on it, and I’ll even care about doing it well, but when I sit down to actually do it, it feels like I hit a wall. Then I’ll put it off — sometimes by doing smaller things that feel productive, sometimes by telling myself I’ll be in a better headspace later — and then later keeps getting pushed.\n\nSo yes, I do bounce around some, but the bigger issue is that tasks that take mental effort feel almost… blocked. Not because I don’t understand them, and not because I think they’re unimportant. It’s more like I can feel the amount of focus it’s going to take, and my brain just wants to avoid it. Then it becomes this whole cycle of delay and pressure and guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships due to your difficulties with attention and task completion?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, not in the sense that my relationships have completely fallen apart or anything dramatic like that. I’m still involved with people, I still show up, I’m still part of things in the community. But it does affect the way people experience me, I think.\n\nThere have been plenty of times where someone is waiting on me to do something or respond to something, and I either forget, or I think I’ll get to it later, and then suddenly it’s the last minute. Then I’m sending these apologetic messages like, “I’m sorry, I meant to follow up,” or “I know I said I’d handle that,” and it’s embarrassing. It makes me feel like I’m putting other people in the position of having to remind me or cover for me.\n\nAt work, coworkers have definitely had to fill in gaps at times when deadlines slipped or I didn’t finish something when I said I would. And with friends, it’s more like… I worry they see me as unreliable, even if they don’t say it that directly. I don’t think people hate me for it, but I can tell it creates frustration, and I feel bad about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve noticed as a pattern.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if a conversation is moving fast or I’m worried I’ll lose my thought, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who constantly talks over them. I’m usually more likely to hold back or wait, especially in work settings, because I’m aware of not wanting to come across as rude.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t ever been formally diagnosed with it before. This is really the first time I’ve sat down with someone specifically to look at it in that way.\n\nAs far as family goes… I’m not really sure. Nobody in my family has talked about having that diagnosis, at least not that I know of. But my family also isn’t the kind of family where people would necessarily go get evaluated for something like that or use those terms, especially older relatives. So I can’t say there’s a known family history. It’s more that I don’t know either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying seated for a long time?\n\nPatient: Not really in a major way, no. I mean, I can sit through meetings or appointments without feeling like I physically have to get up and leave. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly pacing or bouncing out of my chair.\n\nThat said, if something goes on for a long time, I might shift around, change positions, maybe tap my foot a little or look for an excuse to stretch, but I always thought of that as pretty normal. It’s not usually the physical restlessness that stands out to me. I’m more likely to look calm on the outside, I think. So if you mean like feeling driven to move or unable to stay seated, I wouldn’t say that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty engaging in activities quietly, such as reading or doing homework?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being noisy or disruptive while doing things.\n\nIf I’m reading or doing something on my own, I’m usually pretty quiet outwardly. I’m not someone who hums loudly or talks through everything or makes a lot of noise while I’m working. I might shift around or sigh or get up to get a drink, but I don’t think people around me would say, “He can’t do things quietly.”\n\nWith reading, if anything, the issue is more internal than external — but in terms of the question you’re asking, like whether I have trouble engaging in quiet activities quietly, I’d say no, not really. I can sit quietly. I may not always be as productive as I want, but I’m not loud or physically disruptive about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with attention, impulsivity, or hyperactivity as a child, aside from what your teachers mentioned about daydreaming and losing worksheets?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, aside from the daydreaming and losing worksheets, the thing that really stands out from when I was little is that whole pattern around schoolwork and homework. Teachers would tell my parents that I was bright, like I understood the material and could do well when I actually did the work, but I was always sort of mentally somewhere else.\n\nAnd the homework thing was a big deal. It wasn’t always that I didn’t do it — sometimes I did it and then just didn’t turn it in, or I’d forget it was in my folder, or it would be crumpled somewhere in my backpack. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night to make sure I had the worksheets, make sure I knew what was due, and make sure it actually got put back where I could find it the next day.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say there were attention-related problems going back to elementary school. I don’t remember being especially hyper or getting in trouble for running around or anything like that, but the forgetfulness, the daydreaming, and needing my mom to stay on top of basic school stuff were definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are fidgeting or restless, even when you are sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I do move around a bit when I’m sitting — like adjusting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, maybe tapping a pen or my fingers if I’m holding something — but I don’t usually experience it as this strong inner restlessness where I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin.\n\nIf I’m in a long meeting or sitting through something that drags on, I might notice myself shifting more, or I’ll want to stand up and stretch, but I’ve always thought of that as kind of normal after sitting too long. It’s not like I’m constantly aware of needing to move.\n\nSo… maybe mild fidgeting sometimes, but not often in a way that feels like a major problem. I think if you asked people who know me, they’d probably say I seem pretty still most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m especially impulsive in that way.\n\nI might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone is going, especially if it’s a casual conversation and I’m trying to be helpful, but I don’t think I’m someone who constantly interrupts people or blurts things out before I can stop myself. In meetings at work, for example, I’m usually pretty careful about waiting my turn, partly because I don’t want to come across as rude or like I’m not listening.\n\nWith decisions in general, I tend to think things through more than act suddenly. Sometimes probably too much, honestly. So if the question is whether impulsiveness is a frequent daily problem for me, I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you are not actively engaged in a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “frequently,” no. I’m trying to think of what that feels like for me, because when I’m not doing something, I can get sort of… mentally uncomfortable, maybe bored or like I should be doing something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as internal restlessness.\n\nLike, if I sit down with no particular plan, I might end up reaching for my phone or turning on the TV or finding something small to do, but I don’t experience it as this physical inability to relax. I’m not pacing around the house or feeling like I can’t sit still. It’s more that quiet time can feel a little aimless unless there’s some structure to it.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not in a strong way. I can relax sometimes, especially if I’m genuinely tired or watching something, but if there’s nothing engaging me, I may get a little unsettled or bored. I’m not sure I’d call it a major problem, though.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in a broad sense. I mean, I’m trying to be honest and not make myself sound more careful than I am, but when I hear “act on impulse without thinking through consequences,” I picture things like making sudden big decisions, saying yes to something major without considering it, spending a bunch of money on a whim, or doing something risky just because it popped into my head. That’s not really me.

If anything, I tend to pause and run things through in my head, sometimes probably more than I need to. I might make small impulsive choices here and there, like grabbing something unnecessary at the store or deciding last minute to put off a chore, but I don’t think that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not usually like I act first and then realize afterward that I caused a problem.

I guess there are moments where I’ll respond quickly in conversation or make a quick call on something routine, especially at work if it feels low-stakes, but I don’t usually feel like I’m out of control or not considering what could happen. So my answer would be no, not often. At least, it hasn’t stood out to me as a pattern.

I didn’t always connect it to what’s happening now, because as a kid you don’t really have words for it, but in elementary school my teachers used to tell my parents some version of, “He’s bright, but he’s always daydreaming,” or “He knows the material, but he’s not turning things in.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets all the time — like they’d be in my desk, or crumpled in my backpack, or just gone somehow — and homework was a whole thing. It wasn’t necessarily that I couldn’t do it. A lot of times I had done it, or partly done it, but then I’d forget to turn it in, or I’d leave it in my folder, or I wouldn’t even remember there was something due unless my mother checked my backpack every night. I’m usually more likely to hold back or wait, especially in work settings, because I’m aware of not wanting to come across as rude.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t ever been formally diagnosed with it before. I don’t remember being especially hyper or getting in trouble for running around or anything like that, but the forgetfulness, the daydreaming, and needing my mom to stay on top of basic school stuff were definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are fidgeting or restless, even when you are sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I do move around a bit when I’m sitting — like adjusting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, maybe tapping a pen or my fingers if I’m holding something — but I don’t usually experience it as this strong inner restlessness where I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin.\n\nIf I’m in a long meeting or sitting through something that drags on, I might notice myself shifting more, or I’ll want to stand up and stretch, but I’ve always thought of that as kind of normal after sitting too long. It’s not like I’m constantly aware of needing to move.\n\nSo… maybe mild fidgeting sometimes, but not often in a way that feels like a major problem. I think if you asked people who know me, they’d probably say I seem pretty still most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Sometimes probably too much, honestly. So if the question is whether impulsiveness is a frequent daily problem for me, I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you are not actively engaged in a task?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “frequently,” no. I’m not pacing around the house or feeling like I can’t sit still. It’s more that quiet time can feel a little aimless unless there’s some structure to it.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not in a strong way. I can relax sometimes, especially if I’m genuinely tired or watching something, but if there’s nothing engaging me, I may get a little unsettled or bored. I’m not sure I’d call it a major problem, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences of your actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a broad sense. That’s not really me.\n\nIf anything, I tend to pause and run things through in my head, sometimes probably more than I need to.
